1221. RICHARD AUTY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 hammer, value 6 d., the goods of William Lock, in a vessel in a a port of entry and discharge.
CHARLES SARGENT . I am a day-watchman belonging to the East and West India Dock Company. On the evening of the 6th of April I noticed the prisoner as he was going out at the West India Export Dock—he had this hammer in his pocket—he said he had brought it into the dock with him; that he had been working against the Garland, at Deptford, and had come from the dock to go on board the Runnimede —she was lying in the south side of the Export Dock, which is part of the port—he said he had been to the Runnimede to see the captain.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did he not take it out of his
pocket, and give it to you? A. No, I took it—he appeared perfectly sober.
WILLIAM LOCK . I am a shipwright, and live in Ridman's-rents, Narrow-street, Limehouse. This is my hammer—I know it by two marks on it—I had been at work on board the Runnymede —I saw the hammer there on Tuesday afternoon about two o'clock, the first thing after I came back from dinner—I lent it to the captain, and never saw it afterwards till I saw it in the officer's possession.
Cross-examined. Q. You have no doubt of the hammer being yours? Q. Not a bit—I do not know the prisoner at all—the hammer is not worth more than 6 d.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18410405-1222]
1222. LOUISA VALENTINE was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 jacket, value 1 l. 15 s., the goods of Edward Cooke.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of Joseph Hawes.
EDWARD COOKE . I am a seafaring man, and live at No. 38, John-street. I have known the prisoner about thirteen months—she was at my lodging on the 25th of March, and drank tea with me and my wife—I sent my wife to pawn my jacket—it was pawned—the prisoner looked at it, and admired it very much, before it went—my wife put the duplicate on the mantel-piece when she came back—we went out, leaving the prisoner in the room—on our return she was gone, and the duplicate also—the jacket is worth 35 s.
Cross-examined. Q. What day was this you say you lost the duplicate? A. Thursday evening—I had the prisoner taken into custody on Saturday evening—I had not be en searching for her in the meantime, because she came into the house and dawdled my wife about—that was on Saturday morning—she took my wife out with her—she was going to look for a lodging—she sent my wife one way, and she cut away another—I am no relation or connexion of the prisoner's.
Q. Where is your father-in-law? A. In London at present—his address is No. 39, James-place—I saw him there last, this morning—I am not aware where James-place is—it is in St. George's in the East—I am not acquainted much with that part—I have been in London two years—I have been with my father-in-law every day this week—I do not know exactly where James-place is; at the end of King David's Fort, you turn along there—I am not acquainted with the largest street near it.
Q. How often has the prisoner asked you where your father-in-law, her husband, lived? A. He was in Germany.
Q. Answer the question directly; how often has she asked you where he lived? ( No answer.)
COURT. Q. Has she often applied to you to know her husband's direction? A. No—I have been frequently applied to by her.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you ever tell her? A. I have told her—I have told her where he used to live in Germany—I have never told her where he lived in England—I did not know that she had any intention of prosecuting him for bigamy—he has another wife—I do not know that he is married to the prisoner—I never heard it—I do not know what brought the prisoner to this country—my father-in-law has only been over here one week—I will swear I never heard that the prisoner was married to him.
COURT. Q. She never told you she was married to him? A. No.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. That you swear; and, to your knowledge, your wife does not know she is married to him? A. I do not know that—she has not heard any thing of the kind; she never told me any thing of the kind.
Q. You have said this was stolen on Thursday; was she not at your house on the Friday, drinking tea? A. Thursday she was at the house, the 25th.
Q. On your oath, was she not on Friday at your house, drinking tea? A. Thursday, the 25th, that is all I know.
COURT. Q. Was she there on Friday also? A. I do not know.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you drink tea with her? A. I do not know—she was not drinking tea with me on Friday—I cannot recollect whether or not she was in the house, but on the 25th she was in the house, and took the ticket—we found out that she had got it on Saturday—I do not recollect that she was with us on the Friday—I was at work.
Q. Was there not a conversation about the duplicate over your tea, (you cannot forget that you know,) between you and your wife, in her presence? Did not your wife say, "Did not you see me put it in a little box on the mantel-piece?" and did not you say, "No?" A. I saw my wife pot it on the mantel-piece, I recollect now—I recollect that my wife pot it on the mantel-piece on the Thursday—she did put it on the mantel-piece.
Q. Did she say that to you? A. I do not recollect what she said to me on the Friday—I will not swear any thing about it—I saw her put it on the mantel-piece on Thursday.
COURT Q. The question is, not what you saw, but what you said; did you, on her remarking that it was put into a little box, say you did not see it put into a little box? A. I said I did see her—I saw it at the time.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you mean to say on the Friday you told her so? A. cannot say that—I answered, my lord, on the Thursday—I have seen her so many different times, that I cannot pitch on that day—I must have seen her—I think I saw her on Friday evening—I cannot recollect whether she drank tea at our house—I cannot recollect whether we took tea or milk.
Q. Why will you not answer a plain question?—did you drink tea with her on Friday at your own house? A. I must consider—yes, I did—I did not say on that occasion that my wife was so stupid that she was likely to have mislaid the duplicate, nothing of the kind—whether I saw her on the Friday or not, Thursday night was the time the ticket was taken—I not take tea with her myself—I do not swear to it—I said, "Not myself," but I did not swear to it—I know that I am on my oath as to every word I am saying.
CHARLOTTE COOKE . I am the wife of the last witness. I recollect the prisoner being at our place on Thursday afternoon, the 25th of March—she called to tea—I went and pledged a jacket at Mr. Hawes's for 7 s., and brought the money back—I laid part out for tea—I put the duplicate on the mantel-piece, in a little pot, a little ornament—I went out with my husband after tea—I left the little boy, my husband's brother, in the room—he is fifteen years and a half old—when I came back the prisoner was gone, and we directly missed the ticket—we were absent about an hour—
on the following Saturday I went to Mr. Hawes, the pawnbroker's, and there got information that the jacket had been redeemed—in consequence of what I told my husband, he took the prisoner into custody—the prisoner was in the habit of coming often to our place, several times, I cannot say how often—I was on very good terms with her.
Q. Has she applied to you at all on the subject of the direction of any body? A. Yes—no—my father lives in London—I do not know exactly in what part of London—I have not been to see him—I have not been with my husband—he has been to see me.
Q. Where did he tell you he was staying? A. He did not think of staying here—he did not say where he was lodging, not to me—he has been here about a month, or thereabouts—it was on a Sunday, I think, that I first saw him, when he came from abroad, about three weeks ago—the prisoner has not applied to me to know where he was—my father's name is Charles Valantine—the prisoner is no relation to him—she is not married to him—she has never lived with him, as I know of.
Cross-examined. Q. Did it never strike you to ask this woman how she went by your father's name? A. No—she said there was one letter difference in the name that altered the name—I called her Valentine—she calls herself Valentine—I did not ask her why—"e," I think is the letter that is different in the name—I asked her about it, because she said her name was Valentine—I asked her why she called herself Valentine—she said there was one letter difference in the name—I had not mentioned any body to her before—I understood her to mean different from the name "Valentine"—J do not know whose name.
Q. How did you become acquainted with the prisoner? A. She was inquiring for my father, and she found my husband out—she had inquired at a person's I know, and found us out—she said she wanted to see my father, if I could tell her where he was—I asked her why she wanted to see my father—she said he had got some papers belonging to her—she did not tell me she was married to him, she told me no such thing—she said he came by her papers in trying for property for her—he has been formerly a military gentleman, he has nothing to do at present—he came here in hopes to do better—he called on me when he first came, that is a month or three weeks ago—he saw my husband—I was not present at the interview between them—my father saw him when he came in—my husband and he were together, that is about three weeks or a month ago—I do not know whether my husband knows where he lives—he never told me that he went to see him—he never told me that he went every day in the week to see him—I have no notion where he lives—I did not ask him where he was living, or going to live, when he came to pay me a visit—the prisoner has asked me several times where he lived, and she has asked my husband—he told her—I did not hear him tell her, but I know he did, because he had taken the direction on a piece of paper—I cannot say whether she took it from him—I saw the direction he took—I did not see that direction yesterday or to-day—I do not know that my husband saw my father this morning—if that is so, I am quite unaware of it—it is some time since I saw the direction last, some months ago—it was a direction abroad—I do not know where my father lives in this country—I never heard my husband ask him—I do not know that my husband knows where he lives.
Q. Did not the prisoner drink tea with you on the Friday night as you
lost the duplicate on the Thursday? A. Yes—the party consisted of her and her son, me, my husband, and his brother—there was some conversation about the duplicate on the Friday night at tea—I said to my husband, "Did not you see me put it into a little box on the mantel-piece?"—(he was saying I was stupid, and had mislaid it)—he did not say, "No, I did not"—he saw me put it in there—he said it was very strange, it must be there, she must have taken it, she was the only person to take it—he said that at tea—I am twenty-four years old—he said she was the only person that was there, and must be the person that had taken it—he did not say that in her presence—she was at tea, but it was not at that time, not in her presence.
COURT. Q. Do you recollect any conversation about this duplicate in the hearing of the prisoner and your husband? A. He spoke of it—he said it was very strange where it could go, and she said, "You will find it by and by."
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Go on, and tell what the conversation was that took place? A. She told me not to go about it—I had mentioned it to her on the Friday morning, when she came in—she said, "You had better not go about it now, for the laws of the pawnbrokers have altered"—I thought she meant something very wrong when she spoke in that way, because she said they would not listen to me, I had better go in three months' time, and she would go with me to get an affidavit for the lost duplicate—I have been in the habit of going to pawnbrokers—I do not know their laws—I thought it very strange the way she spoke—I told the Magistrate what she said about the laws of the pawnbrokers, the affidavit, and the three months.
COURT. Q. You thoroughly understand the question; when before the Magistrate, you told him about this conversation at tea on the Friday, and about the laws of the pawnbrokers being altered, and what she said to you? A. Yes.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. If I take yon rightly, it was not at the conversation at tea on the Friday that this took place, but on the Friday morning? A. Yes, Friday morning, when she came in to me—it was on the Saturday morning, that she took it out, that she advised me not to go to the pawn-broker's—I have worn mourning for about a month past—my father visited me on his arrival, and that is about three weeks or a month ago—he has never come to our house since, that I am sure of—he has never called but once—my father has not been to see us more than once since his return to London, that I know of, and that is three weeks or a month ago—I do not know any reason why he has never been since.
Q. Did it not strike you as strange that he should call once so long ago, and never call on you again? A. No, I never thought any thing of it—I do not know from any husband that he has ever seen him from that day to this—I do not think he has seen him—he has not told me that he has seen him—he never said that it was very odd he had never seen my father since—my husband is a seafaring man—he was formerly a midshipman—he has not been a midshipman since we were married—we have been married two years and a half—he gets his living in the Docks—he is a labourer in the Docks, in the tea department, carrying things backwards and forwards—he gets half-a-crown a day.
Q. Has not this poor woman, out of her small income, occasionally relieved you and your family? A. She brought me once a little tea when I
was ill, and a little sugar, nothing else—she never did that but once—she never gave me any other things—I could not forget it if she did—she gave my husband a pair of trowsers about nine or ten months ago—it was a pair that was left off of her son's—they were good enough to work in—I am sure she gave us nothing else—I understand the prisoner is a French woman—my father came from Germany last—he had not been to Belgium, as I know—the prisoner has lived at a great many places—she was continually moving—she lived last at the corner of Farnham-street.
COURT. Q. She drank tea with you on the Thursday, did she not? A. Yes—she was there again on the Friday morning, and she was there to tea on Friday—she came on Saturday morning—she did not have any thing then—we missed the ticket on Thursday after she bad left—the jacket was taken out of pawn on Saturday morning—I found it out about two hours after it was taken out—I found it out by going to the pawnbroker's—he told me it had been taken out by a person in black—he described the person—I saw the prisoner again that evening—the policeman and my husband were looking for her, and found her—I was not with the officer when he took her—I understood that the difference between the prisoner's name and ours was that there were two e's in her name, and only one in ours—bars is Valentine, and ours Valantine—I became acquainted with the prisoner by her telling me she knew my father—that was before his arrival in this country—she said he had been a great friend to her—the prisoner has a son, whose father is dead.
Q. You got so acquainted with her, that not only she, but her son, came to your house? A. Yes—I cannot say whit way of life her son is in—she told me my father had got some papers belonging to her, and wished me to get his direction, and she would write—my father's direction abroad was, I believe, given, to her—I did not see it given—I have not seen her since my father has been in England.
Q. Why, this happened on the 6th of April? A. Yes—I did not tell her on the day I pawned the jacket, or any other day, that my father was in England—I had no reason for not wishing her to know his direction.
Q. Had you been cautioned that he did not wish to have any thing to say to her? A. He said he did not want to have any thing to do with her, or to see her, and in consequence of that I never told her he was to be found in London.
Q. How came you to mention the woman's name, or he to you? A. When my father came I said there had been a lady inquiring for him, and mentioned her name, Valentine—he did not say any thing.
Q. You said just now he did not wish to see her? A. Yes, but he said nothing when I mentioned the name—he said, "I do not wish to see her"—considering he had some reason for that, I did not let her know he was in London—I did not know of her claiming to be his wife—I never heard her say any thing of the sort—I never understood it from my husband, or any one else—I did not ask my father why he did not wish to see her—he did not tell me any more than that he did not wish to see her—I thought it was very strange of my father—I thought he had his reasons for it, and did not tell her where he was—he did not tell me his direction—I told him directly he came in that a lady named Valentine had been inquiring for him—he did not tell me he was aware of it—I had no quarrel with the prisoner before I missed the duplicate.
WILLIAM ASHFORD . I am shopman to Mr. Hawes, a pawnbroker, in High-street, Shadwell. On Thursday, the 25th of March, a jacket was pawned by the prosecutor's wife for 7 s.—on the following Saturday it was taken out by the prisoner between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning—she produced the duplicate, paid 7 s. 1 1/2 d., and took it out—I have both the duplicates here—people often come to redeem things which they have not pledged themselves—when the prisoner brought the duplicate, she said, "Make haste, I am in a hurry;" and as soon as it came down, I gave it to her, she paid me the money, and took it away—nothing further passed—she did not mention her name—she was dressed in black—I have not the least doubt of the prisoner being the persons—the jacket is worth 20 s. in the trade—it would cost the man about 2 l. I should think—it was a beautiful jacket.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen her at all before you went to the Magistrate? A. I saw her, on Saturday night, in our shop, with the policeman and her son—I had never seen her before she redeemed this jacket—there was another female in the shop at the time—no one else—on one belonging to the shop—when the prisoner, came in on Saturday night, she made a face as if she wanted to disguise herself—we do not ask questions of persons redeeming an article, if they produce the duplicate—when I saw the prisoner on Saturday night, I did not say she was the person—I said, I did not know—she made such a face, nobody would have known her—I knew her on Monday morning—I said, on Saturday night, I did not think she was the woman, from the dreadful mouth she made—she shut her eyes, and made a face, and that she did all the time she was in the shop—she was brought to me to be identified—she had no teeth out of her head when she took the jacket out—the policeman has three teeth in his possession, which he found in her pocket.
JOHN CROXFORD ( police-constable K 307.) I apprehended the prisoner on Saturday evening, the 27th of March—when charged with this, she said she knew nothing of it—I found 22 s. 6 d. in money on her—I took her to the pawnbroker's, about ten o'clock, to be identified—I did not perceive any person with us—I was standing behind her in the shop, and did not see her countenance—I put her into the box, for the pawnbroker to look at her—he did not express much difficulty in identifying her—he did a little—he said, he could tell better by daylight—he did not undertake to say it was her at that time—I did not find any thing on her afterwards—a female searched her, but I was not present—I have a purse, containing some teeth, which the sergeant delivered to me—I did not see them found—the sergeant is not here.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go to the prisoner's lodgings? A. Yes—I was ordered there by Mr. Cooke—the prisoner did not know I was going there—I searched them—I found no jacket.
WILLIAM COOKE . I am fifteen years and a half old—I was at my brother's house on the Thursday, when the prisoner was there—my brother and his wife went out, and me and the prisoner remained—I did not see her take any thing—no one came into the room till my brother came back—the prisoner went away about half an hour after they were gone—I did not touch any duplicate—I saw there was one there, when my sister put it into a little gallipot on the mantel-piece—when my brother and his wife came home, the duplicate was missed, and the loss of it mentioned—I was there again the next night, Friday—the prisoner was there—her son was
not—he was not there on the Thursday—Mrs. Valentine, myself, my brother and his wife, were there on the Friday—nobody else—I am sure her son was not there—I remember the loss of the duplicate being mentioned in the prisoner's presence on the Friday—she said, there was plenty of time yet to go and see about it; three months was plenty of time to go and stop the ticket, because there was a new regulation in the law—she said, she could go and get an affidavit to get another ticket—my brother had told my sister to go about it in the morning, which would be Saturday morning.
Cross-examined. Q. When was it that this conversation took place, about the affidavit, the three months, and the law being changed? A. On Friday—I cannot tell exactly at what time—it was in the evening part—I cannot say exactly whether it was at tea-time; it was about four or fire o'clock—my brother and sister, me and the prisoner, were present.
Q. Did you see Valentine, that came from Germany, when he came to visit you? A. Yes, I remember his coming to see us—it is about three weeks ago since he came from Germany—I saw him—my brother and sister were at home—they were greatly surprised to see him—I live with my brother and his wife—I was not present exactly when he came in, I was out with the child—I came in just after—I was there along with him, and my brother and sister were there—I recollect my sister telling him that there was a lady wanted very much to see him, a friend of his—he did not ask who she was; he seemed to know who she was—I did not hear exactly what he said—I did not take notice.
Q. When did you see him next? A. He slept in the house that night—I saw him next in the morning—he breakfasted in the house—I did not hear him talk at all about the lady at breakfast—he was there all day, and slept there the second night—it was not at the time the ticket was taken—he came from Germany after the ticket was taken away, and after the prisoner was taken up—three: weeks after—he was not at the house when the ticket was taken—he came there since—a good bit since—three weeks since the ticket was taken—he came from Germany—he has been to Germany twice.
COURT. Q. After he came and staid these two nights, did you lose sight of him? A. No, he slept and staid there all along—he has staid there ever since, and lives in the house—he is living there now.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You left him there this morning, did you not? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Left who there? the man that came from Germany? A. Yes, Mr. Valentine—ray brother and sister, Valentine and I, did not breakfast together before we came out this morning—we dined together yesterday, at my brother's house—he lives along with my brother—Mrs. Cooke dined with him yesterday—I dined at the table, and the day before.
COURT to EDWARD COOKE. Q. Now you have heard what your brother has said, how do you explain it? A. Explain what my brother has said, do you mean?
Q. Yes, about this man Valentine living in your house from the time be came to the present day? A. That is all right—I told you I had seen him this morning, at No. 39, James's place.
Q. Why conceal from the Court the fact, that this man was living in your house, and represent that you had been to visit him every day for the last week?—what did you mean by making such a false and wicked representation
to the Court? A. I did not say he was not living with me—I had been there every day, I said.
COURT to CHARLOTTE COOKS. Q. What did you mean by representing that you had seen your father but once; that he had called but once, when it now turns out that he is actually living in the house? A. Because he told me not to mention about it; he said, he did not wish the lady to know where he was—he did not tell me not to tell the Court where he lived, but not to tell the prisoner.
Q. But when examined on oath, how came you to presume to tell the Court and Jury that you had not seen Mr. Valentine since the day he railed on you, and that you did not know where he lived, when he is living in your own house—how do you reconcile that? A. I do not know, Sir.
NOT GUILTY .
(The witnesses Edward Cooke and Charlotte Cooke were committed.)
[trialAccount t18410405-1223]
1223. SARAH READ was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 shirt, value 4 s.; and I flannel-shirt, value 2 s.; the goods of John Matthew and another: and JANE MELON , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, & c.
JAMES FREDERICK SYMONS . I am in the service of John and Benjamin Matthew, pawnbrokers, in Liquorpond-street. In the afternoon of the 5th of April, the prisoner Read came to our shop and pawned an apron—about an hour afterwards a policeman came and produced a linen shirt and a flannel shirt, the property of my employers, which I had seen banging up in the shop close by where Read stood, about a minute before she came in—she came alone—I did not miss them till the policeman produced them.
Read. I found them on Eyre Street-hill, and there is mud on the shirt, where I trod on it. Witness. I do not observe any mud on it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you observe any one watching or waiting outside? A. No.
GEORGE WATERLOO HOLLAND ( police-constable Q 23.) I took the prisoners into custody—in the station they were both sitting together, shuffling about, and during the time I took Read to be searched by a female, one of them, I cannot say which, put this linen shirt on the table—if I have said it was Melon, I cannot stick to it—there was no one there but the prisoners to put it there—the sergeant on duty asked Melon if it was her property—she said no, she knew nothing about it—I was ordered to take Read to the cell to be searched by a female—on coming back I found this flannel-shirt under the seat on which they were sitting, which put it there, I cannot say—when the sergeant asked Melon if the linen shirt was hers, she said it was not, it was Read's—Read said it was hers—the sergeant asked whether it was marked—Melon said it was her sister's husband's shirt, and Read said it was hers—Read said it was not marked—Melon said it was marked G. B.—the searcher is not here—it was the flannel-shirt that Melon said she knew nothing about.
JAMES HAHMAN . I am shopman to Mr. Watt, a pawnbroker, in Ex-mouth-street. In the afternoon of the 5th of April the prisoners came to our shop in company together—Melon offered this linen shirt in pledge, but would not take what I offered on it—I observed a flannel under her arm, rolled up, which I believe was a flannel shirt—I did not look at the linen shirt enough to identify it again.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410405-1224]
1224. SARAH READ was again indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 flannel-petticoat, value 1 s. 3 d., the goods of Robert Watt.
JAMES HARMAN . I am in the employ of Robert Watt. On Monday, the 5th of April, about five o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came with Melon, and offered some irons in pledge—I noticed one of them snatch down a petticoat, but they stood so close together, I could not say which it was—I saw Read with the lower part of her person, raise the petticoat, and put it under her arm—I sent a lad for a policeman, who came, and I then said to the prisoner, "Give up that petticoat"—they both cried out, "What do you mean? what petticoat?"—I said, "The petticoat under your arm"—she immediately dropped it from under her arm, and was taken into custody.
Prisoner. I never saw the petticoat—it was never in my possession—there were other persons in the shop. Witness. There was one other person at the other end of the shop—I saw it drop from her person.
GEORGE WATERLOO HOLLAND . I am a policeman. I was fetched, and saw the prisoner drop the petticoat from under her left arm, under her shawl—I saw it distinctly fall from her—I picked it up, and took her into custody.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18410405-1225]
1225. THOMAS LEONARD was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 2lbs. weight of bacon, value 1 s. 1 d., the goods of George Waistell.
GEORGE WAISTELL . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Asylum-terrace, Chelsea. On the 27th of March, at half-past nine o'clock, in consequence of information, I went out and stopped the prisoner going in a direction from my shop—I brought him back, and accused him of stealing a piece of bacon—he took it out of his basket, threw it down, and said, "There it is, do the best you can"—I found a piece of that description missing from my window—this is it—I can swear to it.
Prisoner. Q. How far was I from the shop when you overtook me? A. About 100 yards—you were running—you did not look back—you had apparently had something to drink, but were not so drunk but you knew what you were doing.
WILLIAM CROFTON LEVER ( police-constable B 113.) I took the prisoner into custody, and received this piece of bacon from the prosecutor—the prisoner had a basket with three more pieces of bacon in it—he said at first he had bought them, and afterwards, that he had had them given to him by his old woman.
Prisoner's Defence. I received the bacon from a woman I lived with in Spain, and who robbed me of my money and all—I accidentally met her in a public-house—she had this basket and the bacon in it—she asked if I had any dinner for Sunday—I said no, and she gave me the basket and bacon—she was intoxicated, and afterwards regretted she had given it me, and said, "Let me have it back again"—I said, "No"—she said, "If you don't, I will be revenged on you some way or other"—I said, "Do your best," and went out of the public-house with it, and ran as fast as I could, without the least idea that the bacon was stolen—I believe the very woman that gave me the bacon gave the prosecutor the information.
GEORGE WAISTELL re-examined. A respectably dressed woman, passing, said, "A man has stolen a piece of bacon from you, and has gone in
that direction"—I could not swear to the woman if I were to see her—she appeared in middling circumstances—the prisoner did not say any thing about any woman at the time, or that the bacon was given to him—from information the police afterwards received) I believe it was a servant in the neighbourhood that gave me the information—he only threw one piece of bacon out of the basket, and that was mine.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined One Month.

NEW COURT.— Tuesday, April 13 th, 1841.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18410405-1234]
1234. JAMES RADBURN and THOMAS DONOVAN were indicted for ripping and stealing 168lbs. weight of lead, value 29 s., the goods of Joseph Fletcher and others, and fixed to a certain building called the Protestant Dissenters' Original Charity School.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of John Robert Smith; and that Radburn had been before convicted of felony.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN ROBERT SMITH . I am master of the Protestant Dissenters' Original Charity School, in Shakspeare-walk, Shadwell. The back entrance
is in Collins-court—there is no thoroughfare through that court—I do not know either of the prisoners—on Sunday morning, the 21st of March, I had my attention called to the state of the school-room, and the lead on it—I missed some lead—on the following morning I fitted some lead with the policeman—it had belonged to that roof—I had charge of the premises—Mr. Joseph Fletcher is one of the trustees.
MARY PALMER . I am the wife of Thomas Palmer, and live in Collins-court. I know both the prisoners—Radburn lodged in the same street as I did—my house is on the right-hand side of Collins-court, and on that side there is a vacant piece of ground—about one o'clock in the afternoon of the 20th of March I went into the court to hang up some clothes—I saw Radburn and Donovan both there tossing with money—there were other persons there—at four o'clock in the afternoon I went out again—I went to the top of the court, and saw the prisoners there still—about seven I went out—I did not see the prisoners then—I went to Farmer-street, and returned at half-past seven—I then saw the prisoners by the empty house adjoining the vacant piece of ground—they were talking—there was no one with them—it was about five or six yards from the school-room—the privy is common to all the people of the court—the prisoners went along and round the privy—I then lost sight of them—before they went round there I heard Donovan say to Radburn, "May I go?"—Radburn said to Donovan, "Go, be b—d, who is to hinder you?"—they then went towards the privy—I went in-doors, and saw no more.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What are you? A. My husband is a sailor—I am quite sure about the persons I saw in the evening—I did not speak to either of them—I was in the court, outside my window—they went towards the vacant piece of ground—there is a low wall, and part of it does not go quite to the end.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. There is first a short wall, and then a high wall? A. Yes, eight feet high—a person getting on that wall could get to the school-house.
MARY DODD . I am a widow, and live in Collins-court, on the left-hand side. On Saturday, the 20th of March, between eight and nine o'clock at night, I went out for some coals—as I came out I saw the two prisoners going out of the court—I followed them—I had seen them tossing in the court that day—in about five minutes I came back with my coals—the two prisoners were then standing just by the archway—I asked what they wanted there—they answered that they were doing no harm, they would not hurt me—there was a light in Mrs. Sullivan's window—it was shining on them—I am quite sure they are the men—I went out of the court, and watched them—they went out of the court, and turned to the left, and went to a truck standing in Farmer-street, about six doors from the entrance of the court—they leaned on the truck, and then they left, and went towards the public-house, on the other side—I went in-doors—while I was in my room, about nine o'clock, I went to the-window, which is opposite the vacant piece of ground, and drew my curtain—I saw Mrs. Sullivan, who lives in the court—she had a light in her hand—I saw the prisoners there, close to Mrs. Sullivan—one of them dropped a bag containing something heavy, close to Mrs. Sullivan—they were standing towards the privy—after that one of them struck her by the side of her head—she screamed out, "Murder"—I cannot say which struck her—they ran away up the court—I went out to Mrs. Sullivan—she seemed to have been beaten by
the side of her head—I saw the bag at her feet—a person came and turned it out—it contained lead, which was produced to the policeman—I looked, to see if there was any more lead about, and two pieces were near the privy door—I am positive these are the two men.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see them go into the public-house? A. No—my house is up in the corner, near the school-room—Mrs. Sullivan was standing near the vacant ground—there is a wall between the vacant ground and my house—I was at my window up stairs—the court is two or three yards wide—I go out washing—I gave information directly.
MARY SULLIVAN . I am the wife of Timothy Sullivan, of Collins's-court. On that Saturday night I went out to the privy—I had got a candle in one hand—as I was going in, a tall man in a bluejacket and trowsers came out—he passed me, and momentarily I was knocked down—I called out "Murder"—they did not drop any thing that I observed—when I recovered myself I found at my feet a bag with some lead—I did not see the face of the man, because he held his head on one side—I was not much hurt—I was struck so as to be senseless.
CHARLES BROWN ( police-constable K 358.) I went on the Saturday night and took possession of the lead—on the Monday I compared it with the roof of the school-house—in my judgment it came from there.
GEORGE WILLIAM GRAVE ( police-sergeant K 11) On Monday morning, the 21st, I took Radburn into custody—I found him in New Gravel-lane—I had known him for some time—I told him I took him for stealing some lead in Collins's-court, Farmer-street—he said, "Farmer-street, I was not there at all after eleven o'clock"—I shortly after apprehended Donovan—I said it was on suspicion of stealing lead in Fanner-street—he said, "I was not down there at all"—I said, "I am given to understand quite different"—he said, "I was there at four in the afternoon."
JAMES READ . I live in Goold-street, St. George's, which is not quite half-a-mile from Collins's-court. I had a truck which I missed at half-past seven o'clock on Sunday morning, the 21st—I saw it safe at six the night before—I had not lent it to any one or permitted any one to take it away—I went to the station a few minutes before nine o'clock on Sunday morning, and there 1 found the truck.
MR. PAYNE called
ELIZA SMITH . My father keeps the Royal Sovereign public-house, in Bluegate-fields. On the Saturday evening, before Donovan was taken, he was at our house from six o'clock until twelve at night—my father was out on business in the City—I was serving in the house—Donovan was sitting having his tea about a quarter to seven, along with his father—they were drinking and smoking together there until ten—his father is a coal-whipper—my father did not come home till after twelve—I am sure Donovan was there all that time—he was there till the house shut at twelve—his sister was there about seven—she stopped till ten, and went home with her father.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were the prisoners taken out of your house on the Sunday morning? A. No—some people have been taken there—I do not know how many thieves have been taken out of my father's house, during the last six months—I have not been romping about with the people here—our house was the Blue Gate, but it has since been altered to the Royal Sovereign—no one was at home but me and my brother—one of the men in the tap-room stopped till my father came home—the prisoner went away at twelve o'clock—Radburn was not there, too—
the pot-boy or pot-man is not here—my brother is not here—I do not know where Collins's-court is—Donovan did not stay in the tap-room all the time—only from six to ten, and then he sat at the door—I was sitting at the door part of the time—I am seventeen years old—I have not kept much company with him, I have seen him before—I do not know Radburn—I never saw him until I came here—I was in and out of the tap-room and parlour—I had no one to take any thing in, but me and my brother.
MARY CONLEY . I am sister to Donovan. I know the Royal Sovereign public-house, in Bluegate-fields—I was there the night before my brother was taken—I went at six o'clock in the evening, and staid until ten—my brother was there—my father was sitting with him when I went in—I left my brother there—he was there all the time.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How comes your name to be Conley? A. I am married—I am twenty-two years old—my brother gets his living at coal work—he works with my father—he generally works from five O'clock in the morning until dusk—he had been working with my father the day before at Mrs. Punshon's—my father has got the dropsy and gout—I went out to buy a piece of meat for Sunday's dinner—I live by Shadwell pier.
GEORGE WILLIAM GRAVE re-examined. I saw Radburn make this statement before the Magistrate—( read)—"I have nothing to say; I must summon witnesses to prove I was in another place."
Radburn. I am guilty of the crime. Donovan is innocent; a man went with me, who is gone to sea.
JAMES M'GILL . I produce the certificate of the prisoner Radburn's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person who was tried.
RADBURN— GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
DONOVAN— GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.

1235. FREDERICK COOLING was indicted for embezzlement.
GEORGE BENNETT . I am a baker, and live in the Kingsland-road. The prisoner lived with me—it was his duty to take bread to my customers, and to receive the money from them—he ought to settle his accounts every evening—if he received 5 l. from Mr. Massey, he has not paid it to me—he did not bring me any account from Mr. Massey—he absconded on that day.
EDWARD MASSEY . I deal with Mr. Bennett—I cannot recognise the prisoner—I paid 5 l. on the 4th of September.
ANN DINNIOAN . I am servant to Mr. Massey—the prisoner came for Mr. Bennett's bill—I know him perfectly—he used to bring the bread—I introduced him to my master to be paid.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know what day it was? A. No, I did not see my master pay him—he never came after that day.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410405-1236]
1236. CHARLES MACKEN was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 1 violin, value 5 s., the goods of Mary Ann Marriner.
MARY ANN MARRINER . I am a widow, and keep a shop. About six o'clock in the evening, of the 30th of March, the prisoner came into my shop and offered me 1 s. for something—I went for change—I then went to the door to another customer—I saw the prisoner moving from the front of the counter to the side, and then he moved back—I saw the end of the
violin under his cloak—he then walked out of the shop, and waited on the step till the child came back with the change—he hand out his hand for the 3 d. change, which I was to give him for the waste-paper—I was going to give him the halfpence and said, "Stop, let me see what you have under your cloak," pulling his cloak on one side—he said, "Don't make a fuss about it, what it the value of it?" or words to that effect—I said it was a great shame for a person of his appearance to come and wrong me—this is my violin.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been paralyzed two years, and have not been right in my head, and have been subject to do such things as that.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410405-1237]
1237. MARY ANN COX was indicted for a misdemeanor.
ELIZABETH TUCKETT . I conduct the National School in Little Guildford-street, Bloomsbury. About one o'clock on the 10th of March, I was at the school—the prisoner came to me and stated that she met two ladies in the square, and one of them was Miss Powell, who was a teacher in the school—she said that Miss Powell met another lady, who was a teacher in the school, but she did not know her name, and described her dress—that one of these ladies bad bought a bottle-jack of her, and requested it to be brought to me, to be left with me, till she should send her servant to fetch it—she said I was to pay her 5 s. for it, and this lady's servant should bring me the money—the bottle-jack was worth 10 d. ort 1 s.—believing she came from the lady, I gave her the 5 s.—I knew Miss Powell very well—I wondered they had not paid for it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You had some doubt about the truth of her story? A. I cannot say that I doubted, but that remark came into my mind—Little Guildford-street leads out of Great Guildford-street—when she first entered the room, she asked for Miss Tuckett—I said I was Miss Tuckett—she said she had met two ladies in the square, that one was called Miss Powell, that she was a teacher in the school, that she wore a merino dress with two flounces, a scarf, shawl, and a black velvet bonnet, and that was her dress (Miss Powell had been with me not five minutes before,) and that she had met another lady who wore a black shawl and a black silk dress, and that they had purchased the jack of her.
Q. If she had come and used the name of Miss Powell, without describing her dress, should you have let her have the 5 s.? A. I very likely should, but the dress confirmed to me that she had evidently seen Miss Powell.
CATHERINE POWELL . I live in Great Coram-street, Brunswick-square. I did not send the prisoner with the bottle-jack—I never saw her till she was before the Magistrate—I did not authorize her to go to Miss Tucket and get two half-crowns.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know this other lady by the description of her dress? A. Yes, Miss Harper, of Henrietta-street—she is a teach in the school with myself—I did not see her that day.
EMMA GAY . I was at the school that day—the prisoner came a bottle-jack, and got 5 s.
GEORGE BOTT ( police-constable E 26.) I took the prisoner, and have the bottle-jack—it is worth 10 d.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Months.

1240. WILLIAM GREEN and SARAH DAVIS were indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 1 ring, value 8 s., the goods of Joseph Butler.
JOSEPH BUTLER . I live in Newcastle-street, Strand—I go about with a box of jewellery. On the 31st of March I was out, and met Davis and three more women—they asked me what I had got to sell—I said, "I don't think you will buy any thing of me"—I then opened my box—Davis took a ring out, and put it on her finger—she asked me what I wanted for it—I said, 8 s. 6 d.—there were some more girls who wanted to handle some more goods out of my box, and I would not let them—I shut the box up—I asked Davis to give me that ring back—she would not give it me—she would have run away, but I laid hold of her shawl—she tried to run away, and did run across the court—Green had been looking in my box as well as Davis, and when I took hold of Davis, Green said to me, "If you don't let that woman alone, I will break your nose for you"—I then let Davis go, and she ran away with the ring, and Green ran after her—the policeman came up, and I told him—I have never seen my ring since.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you ever go by the name of Goldfinder? A. No, I have always gone by the name of Joseph Butler—I am a native of Poland—Green did not interfere with me till I took hold of Davis.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you been in Newcastle-court? A. Yes—I know two or three women there—I do not know Cowan or Douglas—I never made presents to any of them—I saw Davis again the next morning, in bed—I knew her again—I said to the policeman, "Let her dress herself, I shall know her in a moment."
COURT. Q. Is this the woman that ran away with your ring? A. Yes, I am sure of it—Green was looking into my box at the time she was.
ABRAHAM RANKIN ( police-constable F 136.) I took the prisoner—the prosecutor identified the man, but could not exactly identify the woman till she was dressed, he then said she was the woman.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you desire the prosecutor to give a description of the woman who robbed him? A. Yes, he said she was a dark girl with small eyes, that she had a buff gown and a black shawl.
Witness for the defence.
FREDERICK BARTON . I live in Gilbert-street—I was in Newcastle-court, and the prosecutor came, and told me he had lost a ring—there were four or five girls about him—I asked him if it was a fair girl, or a dark one, that took it—he said he did not know, but she had a dark shawl on.
NOT GUILTY .

1245. JAMES BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 watch, value 5 l.; 2 seals, value 2 l.; 1 watch-key, value 10 s.; 1 ring, value 9 s.; and 1 watch-guard, value 1 s.; the goods of William Vassingham.
WILLIAM VASSINGHAM . On the 2nd of April I was at the Two Brewers public-house, at Greenwich, at twenty minutes past ten o'clock at night—the prisoner was there—I was about treating some old pensioners—I put my hand into my pocket, and found I was without the means of doing so—I was a little the worse for liquor—I had, no doubt, spent the money—I took the prisoner for the waiter or potman—I gave him my watch, asked him to take it to his master, and ask him to lend me 10 s., and I would send for it in the morning—he did not return, and I went to the bar to make inquiry—the landlord and I had a contention about it—I afterwards went out, and met the prisoner in the street—I challenged him with having taken my watch—he denied it on his oath, and words took place between us—a policeman came up—we went back to the house, and my watch was found in his left-band trowsers-pocket.
Prisoner. The gentleman asked me to take his watch, to get some money on it; I did so, and as I was returning to tell him I could not get any thing on it he met me. Witness. My impression is, the prisoner was so drunk he did not know what he was doing; I was not sober; I think he did not mean to steal it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410405-1246]
1246. GEORGE WRIGHT and WILLIAM LAKE were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 1 basket, value 3 s. 9 d.; 3 planes, value 15 s.; 2 saws, value 5 s.; I axe, value 3 s. 6 d.; 3 chisels, value 3 s.; 2 mallets, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 hammer, value 1 s.; 4 gauges, value 1 s. 2 d.; 1 gimlet, value 3 d.; 1 bradawl, value 2 d.; and 50 yards of cord, value 3 s.; the goods of Thomas White.
THOMAS WHITE . I am a carpenter, and live in Bridge-street, Green-wich. On the 25th of March I left a basket of tools with Mrs. Wear, the landlady of the Lord Duncan public-house, in the Broadway—I left
them in the cellar—I know the prisoners well—they were coming into the house at the time I got permission to leave them there—I afterwards went with them in to the tap-room, bad some beer, and paid for it—we all three started towards London, and parted near Bermondsey-street—we were going to Mr. Marshall, the agent for emigration—I was to meet the prisoners again, and waited for them at the turnpike, about half a mile from where I had left my tools—they did not join me, and I went to the Lord Duncan, and found my tools were gone—I did not see either of the prisoners again till they were in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. I believe you had all three been in the employment of Mr. Clarkson, a builder? A. Yes, for several months—I had been at two public-houses with the prisoners before in the space of twelve months—the last time was about a week before, and a week before that—I think I was drinking with them at the Railway tavern on the 24th of March—Wright pawned his own shovel, as he told me, to pay for beer—he went home to my lodging that night, and staid all night—I did not awake rather thirsty next morning, and want some beer—I did not send Wright to sell a piece of lead for 3 d. to get beer—I gave him some lead to get something to eat for himself, not to get beer—we went to the Roebuck public-house—I gave him a stock and bit to pawn that morning, as he said he was very badly off, and had nothing to eat—that was at Deptford, not in the public-house, it was the same day that we went to the Lord Duncan—I was going to Sydney, and they talked about going also—I left them to go to the Victualling-office, and agreed to meet at the turn-pike to go home together—I live at Greenwich, and they at Deptford—I had told them if I could do any thing for them, I would tell them when I met them.
HENRY THURLOW . I am a servant at the Lord Duncan public-house, On the afternoon of the 25th of March the prisoners were there, between three and four o'clock, I was rung for to fetch up the basket of tools from the cellar—I brought them into the tap-room, and delivered them to the prisoners.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) On the night of the 25th of March I stopped the prisoners on the Greenwich-road—Lake had a basket—I asked what he had got in it—he said a few tools belonging to his mate—I found it contained two mallets, two chisels, two gauges, one oil-stone, one gimlet, one brad-awl, one hammer, and a file—I asked Wright if the tools belonged to him—he made no reply—I asked him several times—at last he said, "Yes, they are mine, and what more do you want?"—I afterwards showed them to White, who claimed them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not Wright tell you they belonged to York, and tell you where he lived, and that turned out to be White? A. Yes—the prisoners had been drinking.
NICHOLAS MULLINS . I am a policeman. I took Wright into custody—I was with Evans, and found some duplicates on Wright—five are for the tools which have been pawned.
JOHN LOVICK . I am shopman to Mr. Nash, a pawnbroker, in London-street, Greenwich. I produce two planes, a saw, an axe, and three chisels, pawned on the 25th of March, about a quarter to nine o'clock in the evening, by a young man named "George Smith, for Thomas White, No. 2, Bridge-street "—the prosecutor claimed them.
THOMAS CROSSLEY . I am apprentice to Mr. Abithel, a pawnbroker, of
Blackheath-road. I produce a saw and plane pawned on the 25th of March, about seven o'clock in the evening, by Lake, in the name of Thomas White, No. 2, Bridge-street.
Cross-examined. Q. White does live in Bridge-street? A. Yes.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410405-1247]
1247. GEORGE WRIGHT was again indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 1 case of drawing instruments, value 4 s., the goods of Thomas White.
THOMAS WHITE . I lost this case of instruments on Thursday morning out of my house—I allowed Wright to sleep at my house on Sunday night, and while I was gone into the yard they were taken out of the closet in my house.
Prisoner. Q. When you gave me the stock and bit out of the basket, are you sure they were not in the basket? A. They were not.
NICHOLAS MULLINS . I found the duplicate of the instruments on Wright, pawned at Martin's, in Tooley-street.
WILLIAM ADAMS . I produce a case of instruments pawned by Lake for 1 s. 6 d., who gave his name as White—he was alone.
Property produced and sworn to.
Prisoner's Defence. When I took them out of the basket, it was all on the same day we were drinking; White had a share of the money in drink.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Weeks.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18410405-1248]
1248. ALEXANDER CALVERT and JOHN BROSNAHAN were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 purse, value 4 d.; 2 watch-chains, value 3 s.; the goods of William James Mero: and 8 sovereigns, the monies of Martin Mero: and MARY MORIARTY , for feloniously inciting them to commit the said felony.
WILLIAM JAMES MERO . I am thirteen year old—I am the son of Martin Mero, a manner, and live in Edward-street, Limehouse-fields. On Thursday, the 18th of March, I went to Greenwich—Palmer went with me—I had nine sovereigns, a half-sovereign, and a watch-chain in my pocket—I stole the ten sovereigns from my mother—I left heron the 17th—we went down to the Greenwich rail-road station, and then went to Deptford theatre, after walking about all day—after we came away we came by an eating-house—I said, "I will go and tell a policeman the crime, and he will take me home"—I did not do so—Palmer said, perhaps we might get a lodging-house—we did get one at the prisoner Moriarty's—a man named Smale took us there—Palmer paid her 1 s. for the two of us to sleep there that night—she took us to an adjoining house—about nine o'clock next morning she opened the door, and let us out—after walking about all day we came back about a quarter to seven o'clock—after a little while Palmer said he was very dry, and sent Mrs. Moriarty out for a pot of beer, and I sent for a pot, then he sent out for another pot—then Mrs. Moriarty said, "We have had enough beer, let us have some gin"—then Palmer sent out for half a pint of gin, and then there were two half-pints more sent for—by this time we were getting pretty drunk—then she said we had had enough gin, let us have some rum, but she did not return with rum, it was some sickly kind of stuff, it was sweet in your mouth, and bitter afterwards—I know the taste of rum—she returned with it in a pint pot, and told Palmer to drink hearty out of it—he did so, and
returned it to her—then she poured me out a glassful, and told me to drink that—Palmer jumped about a little while, and fell fast asleep, and was sick—then she said it was almost time to go to bed—I was rather sleepy—she put us to bed—I was so drunk I could not put myself to bed—Calvert carried Palmer up to bed—Calvert was only there when the gin and rum were sent for—there were three men and three girls there when the gin was being sent for, and some part of the beer, and so were Brosnahan and Smale—I was sober enough to see and know them—Brosnahan, Smale, and Calvert were there when we had the gin—Calvert carried Palmer into bed, and I walked in—Brosnahan and Smale went in with us—I do not know where Mrs. Moriarty was when we went to bed—I put the money between the two sheets—it was in a little leather purse—I do not think any body saw me do it—I went to sleep—we. went to bed between one and two o'clock—I awoke before daylight, felt for my money, but found it was gone—I fell off to sleep again—Mrs. Moriarty afterwards opened the door—I told her immediately she came in, and she said, "Oh nonsense, come and have some breakfast," and I went and had some breakfast with her—she never asked me for the money to pay for it—I have never seen the bag or money since.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You stood all this drinking very well, you were quite able to see and know what you were about, when you got into bed? A. Not quite—I had my senses a little—my mother works as a tailoress—I dare say this was some little money she had saved up—I took it from underneath her bed, from between the two beds.
COURT. Q. What did Mrs. Moriarty say to you? A. When she was washing Palmer's trowsers, she called me back, and told me if any body asked me if I was robbed, I was to say no—I did not make any answer, but walked away—the witness Potter told me to go and call a policeman—I walked out, but could not see one—I came back again, and sat there all day, till six o'clock, till Bovis the policeman came, and told me to go to the station.
RICHARD SMITH LAWRENCE PALMER . I went with Mero to Greenwich that day—we went to the Deptford Theatre, then went to Mrs. Moriarty's, and had spirits, beer and rum—Mero and I had a watch-chain a piece—we gave 3 s. 10 d. for the two, which we paid for out of the money Mero took from his mother—I saw Mero with ten sovereigns—he had got them in some paper when I first saw them—we bought two purses each—after having the rum we went to bed—they carried me into bed—I was drunk and sick—I do not know what became of Mero—I cannot say at what time I awoke in the morning—it was light—Mero said he had lost his money—we went to Mrs. Moriarty—she told him not to mind, it was nonsense—I have never seen the chains or money since.
HARRIET POTTER . I am single, and lodge at Mrs. Moriarty's—it is a house of ill-fame—I remember Calvert, Brosnahan, and the two boys being there on the evening in question, and the beer being brought in—I and Shore went up stairs with the boys—they were taken in the adjoining house by Mrs. Moriarty, and while she was gone to take the two children, I and Shore went up stairs to bed—I saw Moriarty go out of one house into the other—there is a communication between the two houses—Mero asked me if my name was not Eliza Payter, as I was very much like a person he knew of that name—I said I was not—he was not very drunk—I saw a sovereign in his hand—he asked me if I thought it would be
safe if he placed it in Moriarty's hands—I said I was rather a stranger there myself, and would rather not have any thing to say in it—Brosnahan came up into the room where I was, and laid on the bed with his clothes on for about five minutes—Calvert came to the foot of the stairs, and I heard him say, "It is all right"—Brosnahan then went down.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you are an unfortunate young woman? A. I am sorry to say I am—I have been so a month.
SARAH SHORE . I am nineteen years old—I lodge at Mrs. Moriarty's. I recollect Mero and Palmer coming—I asked Moriarty whether she had such little children as them lodging in her house—she said yes, she would rather trust them than me—Palmer was dancing with Moriarty, and after that the boys wanted to go to bed—Moriarty took a light and showed them into the next house—I slept with Potter—Smale and Brosnahan afterwards came into our room—Calvert came to the foot of the stairs and called them down—on the Thursday night I heard Calvert say he bad no money, and ask Mrs. Moriarty to trust him.
ARTHUR SAUNDERS . I am bar-boy at the Cricketers' public-house, in King-street, Greenwich. On Saturday morning, the 20th of March, about half-past five o'clock, Calvert and two others, who I do not know, came into the tap-room, and had some ginger-beer and ale—they gave some money to an old collegeman, who was waiting on them—I do not know what money they gave him—it was silver or copper, not gold—about half-past eight or nine, Calvert came in by himself, and had a bottle of ginger-beer—he threw down a sovereign—I was rather surprised, and asked him if he bad any more of that sort—he said he had had one—our potman, who was standing by, asked him to stand something—he said he might have what be liked, and he had a bottle of ginger-beer—I observed that he had a red-scarf on his neck, which I understood him to say he had just bought for 3 s. 6 d.
GEORGE BEAVER . I am a shoemaker, and live in Greenwich Market. On Saturday, the 20th of March, about seven o'clock, Calvert and Brosnahan came to my shop and had a pair of shoes each, for which Calvert paid me a sovereign.
HENRY JULIE . I am shopman to Mr. Cadwell, a linen-draper, in Greenwich. On Saturday, the 20th of March, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, Calvert bought a scarlet handkerchief of me, for which he paid two half-crowns.
JOHN DOWSON . On Saturday morning, about nine or ten o'clock, Calvert came with others and redeemed a coat and handkerchief, which came to 8 s. 3 d.—he gave me a sovereign.
WILLIAM HOLMES . I keep the King's Arms, Greenwich. On Saturday morning, the 20th of March, about a quarter-past seven o'clock, Calvert and Brosnahan came to my house and had some beef-steaks—Calvert changed two sovereigns and Brosnahan one—Culvert showed a scarf to Brosnahan, and said he had bought it for a crown—I saw more gold in Brosnahan's hand when he changed the sovereign.
JAMES OAKE . I am a hatter at Greenwich. On Saturday, the 20th, between eight and nine o'clock in the morning, Calvert came and introduced Brosnahan as a gentleman that wanted a hat—Brosnahan paid me a crown, two half-crowns, and a shilling—Calvert had a hat at the same time, for which he paid two half-crowns, and I saw some gold in his hand at the time.
HENRY YOUENS . I live at Greenwich. On Friday night, the 19th of March, about eleven o'clock, I saw Calvert—he asked me for 1 d. to get half a pint of beer—I gave it him, and he went over to the White Hart public-house and had it.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. Conductor to an omnibus— I know Calvert by working about the flys and vans—he has worked for Mr. Stacey, a fly-master.
EDMUND BURKE . About half-past eleven o'clock on this Friday night, I was standing at the White Hart public-house—Calvert came over from the Mitre, and asked me for a halfpenny for a pipe of tobacco—I gave it him.
JOHN BOVIS ( police-constable R 139.) In consequence of information, I went to Moriarty and asked her who the parties were that were drinking there that night—she pointed to a young man and woman sitting there, and said there were two other young women that were there, but were not at home then—I asked her if she was sure there was no one else—she said, "Yes"—I asked her was not Jack and Alic there—(meaning Smale and Calvert)—she said, "Yes, they were all there"—I asked her who fetched the drink for the boys—she said she did—I asked her when the boys complained to her in the morning, whether she went out to tell a policeman—she said, no, she did not—I then asked Brosnahan who put the boys to bed—(Brosnahan was present)—he said, nobody, his mother took a light as far as the door.
JOHN SIZER ( police-constable R 103.) I took Calvert on Monday the 22nd—I found on him a new hat, and a new pair of shoes—I know Brosnahan to be an associate of Calvert's, they frequent Moriarty's house—I have seen them about the neighbourhood, and I saw them the morning after the robbery, drinking at the Mitre public-house, where I was—he is Moriarty's son, or passes as her son.
Brosnahan. Q. Where have you seen me associate with this man? A. I have seen you in company with him by Skelton's-lane, and likewise in front of the church—I have frequently seen them pass together.
CHARLOTTE MERO . I am the wife of Martin Mero, and live in Edward-street, Limehouse-fields. I lost ten sovereigns on Wednesday night, the 17th of March.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Palmer? A. He is a neighbour's child.
JAMES HARGRAVE , I apprehended Palmer—he told me he had been robbed at Greenwich, and I took him to the office—I had bills printed to apprehend the boys.
Cross-examined. Q. What were the bills about? A. The two boys having left their home; Palmer told me of this instantly I took him.
WILLIAM JAMES MERO re-examined. Moriarty went for every thing we had to drink, except half a pint of beer, and half a pint of gin; I and another young man went for that—I do not know when it was that Calvert and Brosnahan came in—Moriarty had been out—they came in soon after she had been out—they spoke very friendly, as if they were acquainted—after we had been drinking a little while, Calvert said, "I will take you in an omnibus, and we will ride all round Lewisham "—I did not see Moriarty in the room when we went to bed, but Brosnahan, Calvert, and Smale were there.
Brosnahan's Defence. I came home in my vessel on Friday evening—
these two boys were sitting by the fire—I bad my supper, and each of the boys had a part of it—they asked my mother whether she would trust them with a night's lodging, and said they were going to Woolwich in the morning to their friends, and would get money to pay her, and they had no money till then—in the morning I and this young man went out together, and had a pint or two of beer together—I said I was going to buy a pair of shoes, and he said he was going to do the same.
Moriarty's Defence. I don't know any thing about the money; the boy brought in a glass of beer, and gave it to the other.
CALVERT— GUILTY . Aged 23.
BROSNAHAN— GUILTY . Aged 22.
MORIARTY †— GUILTY . Aged 62.
Transported for Seven Years.

1250. BRIDGET MARY BLAKE was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 1 crown, and 2 half-crowns, the moneys of George Luff, from his person.
GEORGE LUFF . I am a pewterer. On the 31st of March I met the prisoner in Church-street, Greenwich, between six and seven o'clock—we spoke, and went to a public-house, and then to a brothel—I gave her a shilling—I was there about a quarter of an hour—I was on the bed with her—I had a crown and two half-crowns in my right-hand pocket, and when I got up I missed my money out of my trowsers' pocket—I had not taken my trowsers off—I charged the prisoner with it—she denied having any money—I went to the window, and called the police.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was that the first night you had known the prisoner? A. I had been in her company previously—I did not know she was married—I had seen her at the Bricklayers' Arms public-house—we bad a pint of ale that night, we then went to the Dover Castle—we had no rum—the prisoner denied having any of my money.
MARY WALKER . I am the wife of a policeman. The prisoner was brought to the station; I searched her, I found a crown-piece under her arm, and she dropped a half-crown on the floor—I do not know where the half-crown came from.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18410405-1255]
1255. JOHN DAWSEY was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, 1 rule, value 5 s.; 6 half-crowns, and 5 shillings; the property of Samuel Barnsley, from his person.
SAMUEL BARNSLEY . I am a carpenter, and live in Beer-lane, Christ-church, Surrey. I was at the King of Prussia public-house on Saturday night, the 3rd of April, at a little after twelve o'clock—I was a little the worse for liquor, but not much—I had six half-crowns, five shillings, and a rule—when I got out I sat down on a step—I could have walked hone if I had been let alone—several people came round me—three people said they knew me, and lifted me up, and one of them took me on his back—when I came a little to my senses I found my shoulder hurt very much, and missed my money and rule—I was thrown down by the party, which made me insensible for an hour—I was not very drunk.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why not get off the man's back if you were so sober? A. I was never carried before—they took hold of my arm to lift me up—nobody took hold of my legs—I told them to let me alone, but they would take me—I knew when I was on the man's back—they would not let me get down—I had about three pints of beer and one glass of gin—I was carried sixty or a hundred yards.
MARIA JONES . I live with my mother and father, in Red Lion-court, Union-street. I was in Gravel-lane on the 3rd of April—as I passed the King of Prussia public-house they turned the prosecutor out, and he sat on the step of a door—he appeared drunk—a mob got round him—he was lifted on a man's back, and carried to the Two Brewers public-house—as they went along I saw the prisoner walk by the side of the man who carried him, and when they got him down to the Two-Brewers they flung him down, and he fell on his shoulder—I saw the prisoner feel in his waistcoat pocket—he did not appear to take any thing out of it, but then he felt in his trowsers pocket, and took something out—I did not see a rule—I had known the prisoner for above twelve months—there were only three about the prosecutor at the time—after that the prisoner went with the other two men to a pork-shop in Gravel-lane—they stood at a window there, and he shared the money between the three—I saw money pass from him to the two—they left the prosecutor lying on the ground, not very far from the pork-shop.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not he able to get up? A. No—he appeared drunk—I think it was all shillings that I saw pass between them—I was going for some beer for my mother's supper when I saw this—my mother came home late, poorly, or I should have been in bed—I mentioned this when I came home—the mob went away—my mother told me the man's name when I described him to her—she said she knew him—I knew him before, but not his name—I told her he bad a blue jacket and canvass trowsers, and then she knew his name by the description I gave her.
COURT. Q. Did you give any further description of him? A. No—ray mother raid his name was John Dawsey—I told her nothing else, but that I had seen him before in Union-street.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where was your mother that night? A. At home—she gave information that I knew who the man was.
RICHARD WHBATLEY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody, from the prosecutor, on Monday the 5th, at the Three Sugar Loaves public-house, Union-street—the prosecutor looked in at the door at the time I brought him out—he said he knew nothing about it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you search him? A. Yes, and found 1 s. 8 d. on him, and a duplicate of a hat.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18410405-1256]
1256. CHARLES KNIGHT and JOHN TAYLOR were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of March, 1 gelding, price 6 l., the property of William Prior.
WILLIAM PRIOR . I live at Battersea. On the 4th of March I had a gelding safe—on the 5th I sent for it, and it was gone—I had turned him out in Battersea Marsh—I found it on the 8th, at Mr. Towell's stables—I know nothing of either of the prisoners.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What sort of place is the Marsh? A. About three hundred acres of land, which is inclosed all round with a large ditch, five or six feet wide, and the stream of a sewer runs round it—I do not know whether the gelding could jump.
COURT. Q. It was of very small value? A. About 6 l.—it cost 6 l. 5 s.—my brother had turned him out.
ISAAC PRIOR . I am the prosecutor's brother. I turned the horse out on the Marsh, on the 4th of March—I missed him on the 5th, and saw him again on the 8th, at Towell's stables in Clerkenwell—I do not know either of the prisoners.
HENRY HUSSEY . I lost a horse myself, which was fed with Prior's, my neighbour—I found them both at Towell's on the 7th of March—I never saw the prisoners till they were in custody.
HENRY TOWELL . I am a horse-slaughterer. Prior's horse was found in my possession, on the 7th of March—it was brought to me by the prisoner Knight, on the 5th of March, about eight o'clock in the morning—there was another horse with it—he offered them both to me—I bought the two for 50 s.—he said he had brought them from Mr. Renton's, soap-maker, Park-place, Walworth, and he must see them slaughtered—I told him to bring them down to my premises—this was on Clerkenwell-green—I told him I could not slaughter them then, for my inspector did not come before ten o'clock—I do not slaughter any horses till the inspector has seen them—he brought the horses down to my place, No. 1, Bowling-street, Clerkenwell—I paid him for them—he said he would come in an hour and see them slaughtered—he did not come, and I had suspicion,
and sent over to Mr. Renton to know if he had sent them—I never saw the prisoner again till he was taken, which was three weeks afterwards—Taylor had hold of the horses' heads on Clerkenwell-green—my dealings were with Knight—Prior came and claimed one of the horses on the 8th—Hussey saw them on the 7th—I delivered them to them.
WILLIAM READ . I am a policeman. I apprehended Knight in James-street, Walworth, on the 27th of March—I told him I took him for horse-stealing—he said nothing.
WILLIAM LEE . I apprehended the prisoner Taylor at No. 12, James-street, Was worth, on the 27th of March, at the same time as Knight—they were apprehended together—I found a pair of scissors in the house, which I produce.
EDWARD HUSSEY . I saw Prior's horse at Towell's, and know it to he his—I saw my father's horse there also.
Cross-examined. Q. You were living with your father at the time you turned the horse out? A. Yes—that was not Prior's horse—I know his horse—I have been in prison once, at Brixton, after I had been tried—it was three or four years ago—it was not for horse-stealing—I was in custody for horse-stealing six or seven years ago—I do not know how this horse got away—I am certain of that.
Q. Was it you first discovered the loss? A. My father went out first, and I went out after him—we went found together.
HENRY HUSSEY re-examined. I know the horse which Towell gave up to be Prior's.
ISAAC PRIOR re-examined. I am quite sure the horse found at Towell's, is my brother's, which I had turned out.
KNIGHT— GUILTY . Aged 22.
TAYLOR— NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1257. CHARLES KNIGHT and JOHN TAYLOR were again indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 mare, price 4 l. 10 s., the property of William Paul Mellish.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM PAUL MELLISH . I live at Wands-worth. On the 24th of March I had a brown mare with three white feet, a white face, and a long tail—I turned her out on Wands worth-common, and fettered her feet to prevent her straying—next morning, at six o'clock, she was gone—I gave information at the station, and on the Friday the prisoner Knight was brought to me in custody, in Smithfield—I went with him to White's slaughter-house in Whitechapel, and found my mare there—the prisoner was allowed to go away—the mare had her tail cut, and her heels trimmed when I found her—she had cost me 4 l. 10 s. four months before.
HANNAH SMITH . I live at No. 18, James-street, Walworth. The prisoner Knight took a house of me on the 20th of March—he was alone—I saw him in the house on the 25th of March, and Taylor was riding a horse up and down the street—he went to the house where Knight was, got off the horse, led it through into the yard, and tied it up—it had a white face—next morning I saw Taylor in the yard, trimming the horse—Knight was present.
BENJAMIN WHITE . I live in Thomas-street, Whitechapel. On Friday
morning Knight brought me the mare in question, and wanted a sovereign for it—he said he came from White-square, Clapham—I asked who recommended him to me—he said, a gentleman in Berraondsey-street—I asked where he lived himself—he said, in White-square, Clapham—I offered him 15 s. for her—he went outside with her, came back, and said he would take 16 s. for her, which I gave him—in consequence of information, I went out afterwards to look after Knight—I found him with Taylor, at Aldgate—they were walking together—I brought him back to my shed.
WILLIAM READ . I apprehended the prisoners together.
KNIGHT— GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
TAYLOR— NOT GUILTY .
