1260. JAMES WILLIAM ALLSHORN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of December, 27 1/2 yards of printed cotton, value 20 s.; and 1 looking-glass and stand, value 20 s., the goods of Frederick Piggott his master; and JAMES EDWARD ALLSHORN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
FREDERICK PIGGOTT . I am an upholsterer, and live in George-street, Richmond. James William Allshorne was living with me as clerk—he came to me in August—on Saturday, the 18th of March, the policeman came and told me he had found some duplicates on my clerk's father, and amongst them one for some printed cotton, and another for some furniture and a looking-glass—he showed me the one for the cotton, which was at a pawnbroker's at Richmond—I went there and identified it—the glass produced is mine, and this is my cotton—one parcel of it was pawned at Richmond; and this is the other part of it—I missed them when I came to examine my stock—James William Allshorn was then living with me—I did not know that he had a father till a few weeks before that.
James William Allshorn. Q. How do you know that to be your cotton? A. By this mark at the back of it, which corresponds with the other piece—I bought two pieces on the 13th of November, and here is the corresponding piece, which has the same mark—I know the glass by a private mark on the back of it, which has been partly erased, but not sufficiently—I believe it is your writing.
WILLIAM WYLLIE . I am assistant to Mr. Edwards, a pawnbroker. This seven yards and a half of cotton were pawned by James Edward Allshorn, the father, on the 23rd of December, in the name of Jones.
PHILIP ROSE . I am assistant to a pawnbroker in Holborn. I produce a swing-glass and some cotton, pawned with me, on the 24th of December, by Henry Allshorn.
STEPHEN CREED ( police-constable V 200.) I went to search the residence of James Edward Allshorn—I saw him throw a paper parcel out of his pocket behind the door—I picked it up, and it contained seven duplicates; and, on searching him, I found the duplicate of the seven yards and a half of cotton pawned at Richmond.
WILLIAM IRELAND ( City police-constable, No. 130.) I found the duplicate of the other cotton and the glass on Henry Allshorn.
FREDERICK PIGGOTT re-examined. When James William Allshorn was locked up, I asked him if there was any thing else he had taken—he said, "Yes, there is the glass, and the other furniture, that is all"—he was going on to state about some money he had kept from me, when the policeman came up, and advised him to say no more.
John William Allshorn. He came and held out an inducement to me that he would be as lenient as possible, and he gave me two hours to consider of it. Witness. No, I told him if I found more property, it might make me more severe—I said, "You had better tell me where it is, it may save me trouble"—he had told me of the glass before, and said he did not pledge it himself.
James Edward Allshorn's Defence. On the 23rd of December I was in London; my son Henry told me be could make me a present of some cotton, to cover some chairs that I had, and I accepted it, not questioning but that he had purchased it, and the glass too, as he said he had.
HENRY ALLSHORN ( a prisoner.) I did make my father a present of it.
JAMES WILLIAM ALLSHORN— GUILTY . Aged 27.
Confined One Year.
JAMES EDWARD ALLSHORN— NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18410405-1261]
1261. JOHN MILLER was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
SUSAN MUCKLOW . I am a widow, and a baker; I live in High-street, Peckham. In the evening of the 12th of March, the prisoner came and asked for a pennyworth of biscuits—he gave me a sixpence—I said it was bad—he said he did not know it—he gave me another—I saw that was bad—I bent them both—he left the biscuits, and went out—I sent my lad after him—in ten minutes he was brought back by a constable—I kept the sixpences in my hand, and marked them, and gave them to the officer—I said to the prisoner, at first, "If I had a policeman here, I would give you into custody"—I kept both the sixpences in my hand till the constable brought him back.
CHARLES WHITE ( police-constable P 57.) On Friday evening, the 12th of March, between seven and eight o'clock, I saw the prisoner in High-street, Peckham, about three yards from Mrs. Mucklow's shop—I did not see him go into her shop, but a lad came out and told me something—I
ing to a gentleman's house, which was no thoroughfare—I followed, and found him standing with his back against a tree—I then took hold of him—he said he had done nothing—I took him back to Mrs. Mucklow's shop, searched him, and found on him one good half-crown, one good sixpence, and 1 s. 2 d. in copper, all good.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. These sixpences are both counterfeit, and both cast in one mould; the other money is all good.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to Greenwich to look for work; I had change for a half-sovereign at a house where I stopped for the evening; I cannot give any account of these sixpences, only I took them at the house where I changed the half-sovereign; I should not have offered them if I had known it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1263. THOMAS DODD was indicted for feloniously receiving of a certain evil-disposed person, on the 9th of March, 1 brush, value 1 s.; the goods of William Metcher; well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
WILLIAM METCHER . I am a bricklayer, living in Ann-street, Waterloo-road. I lent a brush of mine to Garland—this now produced is it—I know it by the irregularity of the hairs, and the handle.
JOHN GARLENT . I had this brush from the prosecutor—I took it to Norwood, where I was plastering—on Monday, the 8th of March, it was missing—I had left it safe on Saturday evening, the 6th, in the fire-place—the house was locked—the jamb was broken from the doorway.
WILLIAM COCKRILL ( police-constable L 108.) I went to search the prisoner's premises on the 24th of March—I found this duplicate there, which led me to the pawnbroker's, where I found this brush—there were thirty-five duplicates belonging to the prisoner.
Prisoner. I purchased it of a person that came to my shop, where I sell carpenters' tools, and other things; I asked what he wanted for it, and he said, 1 s.; I gave it him; and, wanting some money, I pawned it at Mr. Beck's, in Blackfriars'-road, for 6 d. Witness. His uncle died a short time ago, and left him some tools, and he opened a little shop—I found this book there, in which there was nothing entered on the 9th of March, when this was pawned—I believe he has no license as a marine-store dealer.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18410405-1264]
1264. WILLIAM KILLICK and JOHN HART were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 241bs. weight of soap, value 10 s., the goods of Benjamin Hawes and others, their masters.
MR. CLAEKSON conducted the Protection.
JAMES BROOK ( Police-constable L 118.) On the 2nd of Apnl, in the
evening, I was at Lambeth—I was not on duty, nor in my uniform—I saw the two prisoners, about a quarter-past eight o'clock, in Stamford-street—Hart was carrying a bundle containing soap in a Macintosh cape—they crossed into the York-road, and went down Button-street, and when they got to the bottom of that I saw Hart give Killick the bundle—I then stopped Killick, and asked what he had—he said, "Some soap"—I asked him where he got it from—he said he found it—Hart then ran away, Goff, who was with me, ran after him and brought him back—Killick owned the Macintosh, and I found in it six bars of mottled soap—I searched Killick, and found in his pockets four half-bars of soap—I found some receipts on him—I found where Hart lived, and went to the house in company with Goff—the prisoners were not going towards his house when I saw them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. This was on Friday evening? A. Yes—the prisoners were in Stamford-street when I first saw them.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 31.) On Friday night I was in the York-road, and saw the two prisoners walking together—I saw Hart run away, and I went in pursuit of him—I ran twenty or thirty yards, and secured him—I found nothing on him—I asked him where be brought the soap from—he said, "From home"—I asked where he lived—he said, close by, and he was going to take the soap to that man's (pointing to Killick)—I afterwards went to the address that Hart gave, at No. 5, Angel-place—he and his mother have one room there—I found two pieces of soap in the cupboard.
JOHN MEAGER . I am clerk to Benjamin, Thomas, and William Hawes. The prisoners were employed by them as carmen, and when they were not employed in delivering soap, they were engaged on the premises—they leave at seven o'clock in the evening—Killick was carrying out mottled soap on the 1st of April, and on the 2nd they had both been carrying out yellow soap—this mottled soap is my employers', and this yellow also—there is a little imperfection in the machine which cuts it, and one side is thicker than the other—that is the case with these outside bars—Hart was employed occasionally in the warehouse as a packer—here is about 28lbs. of soap, worth about 13 s.
Cross-examined. Q. About how much soap in the course of a year is cut by this machine, which is imperfect? A. Several hundred tons—this machine is always used, and all the soap sold by us has the same mark—the mottled soap sent out by Killick on the Thursday had the same imperfection—he had two small parcels to deliver that day, one at Lambeth and one at the London Hospital—about 1 3/4 cwt. in all—neither of these customers are here to-day—we have had Hart for several years—I believe his father was there for thirty years—the gate-keeper is there till eight o'clock in the evening.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is there any other circumstance except the outside bars that you can speak to? A. The mottled soap was not a good boiling—the mottle did not come out well, and the mottle in this soap is not out so well as we could wish—there was soap of this boiling in the warehouse.
WILLIAM WATSON . I am groom to one of the Messrs. Hawes. I know this Macintosh—I have seen Killick wear it—he told me he made it himself.
JOHN LYNCH . I am gate-keeper at Messrs. Hawes. The two prithen followed the prisoner—he crossed the road, and went up a walk lead-soners
left their premises, on the 2nd of April, between half-past seven and eight, o'clock.
KILLICK— GUILTY . Aged 31.
HART— GUILTY . Aged 18.
Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18410405-1265]
1265. AUGUSTA LAY was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 1 spoon, value 4 s. 6 d.; part of a brooch, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Edward Dutton, her master.
EDWARD DUTTON . The prisoner was my servant—she came to me on the 2nd of March—I saw this spoon safe on the 28th of February, the day before she came—I missed it on the last day of February, or the 1st of March—I supposed the other girl had lost it—I saw nothing of the spoon till the 15th of March, when the policeman brought it—this brooch is also mine—it was safe seven or eight days before—it was found on her—I swear it is my property.
ELIZABETH DUCKETT . I am the wife of Joseph Ducket, a policeman. I searched the prisoner one Monday—I do not know when—I found 9 s. 6 d. on her, and the brooch in her bosom.
JAMES UPJOHN . The prisoner came to my shop on the 15th of March, about three o'clock in the afternoon—she offered this spoon for sale—I detained it and her.
CHARLES JACKS ( police-sergeant T 20.) I took the prisoner in charge.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410405-1266]
1266. AUGUSTA LAY was again indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; 5 pieces of silk, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 pocket-book, value 1 s.; 1 printed-book, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 fork, value 6 d.; 1 brooch, value 6 d.; the goods of John George, her master.
JOHN GEORGE . I live at Richmond. The prisoner was in my service for about seven weeks—she left me on the 1st of March—on the 15th I was told something, and missed the things stated—these now produced are mine.
CHARLES JACKS ( police-sergeant F 20.) I searched the prisoner's box, and found these articles in it.
GUILTY. Aged 16.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. —
Confined Nine Days.
[trialAccount t18410405-1267]
1267. ROBERT LOOSELY was indicted for receiving, on the 31st of March, 21bs. weight of marjoram seed, value 14 s., the goods of James Richard Tyler, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JAMES RICHARD TYLER . I am a seeds-man, living at New Brixton. I bought 3lbs. of marjoram seed at Mr. Sangston's—I sold 1lb. of it, and lost the other from the seed room—it was in a paper bag—I believe this now produced to be mine.
ALEXANDER ANDERSON LANGSTON . Here is my writing on this bag, the same as there was on the bag I sold to Mr. Tyler, and the name of its seed "knotted marjoram."
EDWARD DENVER . I am a seedsman at Brixton. On the 31st of March, the prisoner came with this seed to sell—I asked him what he wanted for it—he said 4 s. or 5 s. a pound—I had known him as being in the habit of buying seed of me and others—I have always considered him very honest—I had the best opinion, of him—I told him the quantity was of no use to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How long have you known him? A. Four or five years—he was an honest, hard working man.
WILLIAM WOODWARD ( police-constable P 157.) I was sent for and took the prisoner—this is the seed—the prisoner said his wife received it from a person he did not know, and his wife told me she received it from a person who has absconded.
NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, MAY 10 TH, 1841.
